Now White has pawns at a4 and b4, and Black has a pawn at a5.
If White plays a4axa5 (capture), then Black plays Ra8axa5.
The a-file is now open and Black has a rook on a5.

So a4a5 is better than b2b4 because it prevents a6-a5.

Is a1-f1 better than a4a5?
a1-f1 prepares f6+.
But a4a5 secures the a-file.
In endgames, limiting the opponent's pawn moves is crucial.

Let's check if there are any other threats.
Black's pawn on g3. If white plays a4a5, and black plays ...Kg5, and then ...Kf4.
The black king is moving towards the g2 pawn.
White's king is at g1.
If the black king reaches f3, he attacks g2.
Wait, if the Black king is at f3, and the black pawn is at g3.
The white king is at g1.
The rook is at a1.
White can play Ra1-f1+ to check the king.

So, there's no immediate disaster.
a4a5 seems like a strong, strategic move. It clamps down on the a-pawn and maintains a flexible position for the rook.

Wait, let me re-read the legal moves one more time.
g1h1, g1f1, a1a3, a1a2, a1f1, a1e1, a1d1, a1c1, a1b1, a4a5, b2b3, b2b4.
Yes, a4a5 is available.

Let's double check if a1-f1 is better.
If a1-f1, it's a move that prepares for activity.
But a4a5 is a move that restricts the opponent.
Usually, restricting the opponent's counterplay is more important.

Another thought:
If White plays a4a5, can Black play ...Ra8-a7 then ...Ra7-d7 then ...Rd7-d2?
Yes.
If White plays a4a5, Black could try to get the rook to the 2nd rank.
But White has the rook at a1.
If Black plays ...Ra7-d7-d2, White can defend or attack.
Actually, if the rook is at d2, it's attacking g2 and b2.
But the rook at a1 can move to d1 to defend.

Let's consider a4a5.
One more thing: is there any reason NOT to move the pawn?
If the rook stays at a1, it guards the a-file.
If white plays a4a5, the rook still guards the a-file.

Let's check for any "invisible" tactics.
White: Kg1, Ra1, Pa4, Pb2, Pg2.
Black: Kg6, Ra8, Pa6, Pg3.
Total material: White (R, 3P), Black (R, 2P).
White is up one pawn.
In rook endgames, a single pawn advantage can be decisive.
White wants to avoid trading rooks if the resulting pawn endgame is losing/drawn, and wants to use the extra pawn to win.
The pawn at a4 is part of a possible passed pawn (with b2).
If the a4 pawn moves to a5, it's closer to promotion.
If White can eventually push b2-b3-b4-b5 and a5-a6, the pawns will be very strong.

So a4a5 not only blocks the black pawn but also advances the white pawn.

Final check: a4a5.
Black's options:
1. ...Kg5 (King activation)
2. ...Ra7 (Rook repositioning)
3. ...Rb8 (Rook repositioning)
None of these are devastating.

If White plays a4a5, and Black plays ...Kg5, White can play a1-f1.
If Black plays ...Kf4, White can play Rf1-f6+ (if the square is empty).
Wait, the rook is at f1, the king is at f4.
Rf1-f6+? No, that's not a check.
The rook is at f1, king at f4. To check, the rook must be on the f-file or the 4th rank.
Rf1-f4 is not possible (king is there).
Rf1-f5+ is a check!
And then the king must move.

This seems like a good plan.
So a4a5 is a great start.
